    Headline
    dyspnea, lactic acidosis, malaise, metabolic acidosis, myalgia
    General Information
    As with metformin monotherapy, lactic acidosis can occur with glyburide; metformin combination therapy. Lactic acidosis is a rare but serious form of metabolic acidosis that can occur if metformin accumulates during treatment; when it occurs, it is fatal in approximately 50% of cases. The onset of lactic acidosis is often subtle, and accompanied only by early nonspecific symptoms such as malaise and myalgia (1% to 5%), and quickly followed by respiratory distress (dyspnea; 1% to 5%), increasing somnolence, and nonspecific abdominal distress. Lactic acidosis is a medical emergency that must be treated in a hospital setting; metformin should be discontinued immediately, and general supportive measures promptly instituted. Prompt hemodialysis is recommended to correct the acidosis and remove the accumulated metformin. Such management often results in prompt reversal of symptoms and recovery. Lactic acidosis is characterized by elevated blood lactate levels (more than 5 mmol/L), decreased blood pH, electrolyte disturbances with an increased anion gap, and an increased lactate/pyruvate ratio. When metformin is implicated as the cause of lactic acidosis, metformin plasma levels greater than 5 mcg/mL are generally present. More marked lactic acidosis/metabolic acidosis may be associated with hypothermia, hypotension, and resistant bradyarrhythmias. The reported incidence of lactic acidosis in subjects receiving metformin is very low (approximately 0.03 cases/1,000 person-years); of nearly 20,000 subjects in clinical trials, there were no reports of lactic acidosis. A nested case-control study of 50,048 subjects with type 2 diabetes mellitus demonstrated that during concurrent use of oral diabetes drugs, there were 6 identified cases of lactic acidosis; all the subjects had relevant comorbidities known to be risk factors for lactic acidosis. The incidence of lactic acidosis in metformin recipients without comorbid conditions appears to be no different from in recipients of other antidiabetic agents. Risk factors include significant renal insufficiency, the presence of multiple concomitant medical/surgical problems (e.g., liver disease, alcoholism, cardiorespiratory insufficiency, or other conditions associated with tissue hypoperfusion or hypoxemia), mitochondrial disease [e.g., mitochondrial encephalomyopathy with lactic acidosis and stroke-like episodes (MELAS) syndrome and maternally inherited diabetes and deafness (MIDD)], and exposure to multiple concomitant medications known to increase risks. The risk of lactic acidosis increases with the degree of renal impairment and age. Lactic acidosis is less likely to occur with metformin than with other biguanide agents (e.g., phenformin), because metformin is not metabolized, does not bind to liver or plasma proteins, and is excreted by active tubular processes. Depending on clinical context, consider evaluation for MELAS, MIDD, or another mitochondrial disease. Regular monitoring of renal function and by use of the minimum effective dose of metformin may reduce the risk of this adverse reaction. Individuals should be informed to discontinue metformin should symptoms suggestive of lactic acidosis appear and promptly report the symptoms to their care team.

    Headline
    anemia, Co-Enzyme Q-10 deficiency, folate deficiency, vitamin B12 deficiency
    General Information
    Less frequent but potential side effects of metformin include vitamin B12 deficiency and the resultant possibility of anemia with chronic use. Patients with inadequate B12 or calcium intake seem to be predisposed to developing subnormal B12 levels. Metformin may also decrease folate levels leading to folate deficiency, although the exact mechanism is unknown. Vitamin B12 and folate concentrations should be monitored during metformin therapy and supplement as indicated. In addition, glyburide inhibits the Co-Enzyme Q-10 NADH-oxidase enzyme and may lead to Co-Enzyme Q-10 deficiency. Supplementation is advisable to avoid adverse reactions.

    secondary failure
    General Information
    The ability of many antidiabetic agents, including glyburide, to effectively lower blood sugar may decrease over time; this process is called secondary failure.     Headline
    flushing, hyponatremia, SIADH
    General Information
    Hyponatremia and the syndrome of inappropriate secretion of antidiuretic hormone (SIADH) have rarely (< 0.1%) occurred in patients receiving sulfonylureas, manifesting as symptoms of water intoxication (e.g., mental confusion, nausea, anorexia, dizziness, decreased sodium concentration, increased urinary osmolality, and decreased serum osmolality). Description
  Text
  Glyburide; metformin is a combination of 2 oral antidiabetic agents. Glyburide is a second-generation oral sulfonylurea. Metformin is an oral biguanide antidiabetic agent. The combination is used as an adjunct to diet and exercise to lower blood glucose levels in adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). While metformin carries a low risk for lactic acidosis as outlined in the prescribing information and boxed warning of all metformin-containing products, care in prescribing and monitoring can reduce this risk. Glyburide is often added to metformin with or without other antidiabetic agents in individuals who need further medication to achieve A1C and blood glucose target goals; the combination of metformin with glyburide results in significant improvements in fasting glucose, postprandial glucose, and A1C versus the use of either agent alone. First-line T2DM therapy depends on comorbidities, individual-centered treatment factors, and management needs. In individuals with T2DM with or at high risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), heart failure (HF), and/or chronic kidney disease (CKD), certain glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) or sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitors with proven benefit are appropriate initial therapy, with or without metformin based on glycemic needs. In individuals with T2DM who do not have ASCVD/indicators of high-risk, HF, or CKD, sulfonylureas are an option that, as a class, generally reduce hemoglobin A1C by 1% to 2%. Disadvantages of sulfonylurea therapy include hypoglycemia (especially in the geriatric adult), weight gain, and frequent loss of efficacy over time; however, the weight gain is relatively modest in large cohort studies and the incidence of severe hypoglycemia is lower than with insulin. Glyburide, due to its prolonged duration of action, is not the preferred sulfonylurea in geriatric individuals; glipizide is considered a better therapeutic choice for the older adult. Glyburide; metformin combination products were initially FDA approved in 2002.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  The use of metformin in combination with glyburide results in improvements in blood glucose that are greater than if the drugs are used as single entities.
•Metformin: Metformin improves glucose tolerance in patients with Type 2 diabetes; resulting in improved basal and postprandial blood glucose. Metformin decreases hepatic gluconeogenesis and decreases intestinal glucose absorption. In addition, metformin improves utilization of glucose in the periphery. Increased cell membrane glucose transport occurs in skeletal muscle and adipose tissue; the effect may be due to improved binding of insulin to insulin receptors. Metformin causes a 10—20% decrease in fatty-acid oxidation and a slight increase in glucose oxidation. Metformin also decreases plasma VLDL triglycerides resulting in modest reductions in plasma triglycerides and total cholesterol. Patients receiving metformin show a significant improvement in HbA1c, and a tendency toward improvement in the lipid profile, especially when baseline lipid values are abnormally elevated. Unlike phenformin, metformin does not normally inhibit the mitochondrial oxidation of lactate unless plasma levels of metformin become excessive (i.e., in patients with renal failure) and/or hypoxia is present.
•Glyburide: The hypoglycemic action of glyburide is due to stimulation of pancreatic islet beta cells, which increases insulin secretion. Administration of glyburide also produces extrapancreatic effects that contribute to its hypoglycemic activity. These effects include reduction of basal hepatic glucose production and an enhanced peripheral sensitivity to insulin secondary to an increase in insulin receptors or to changes in the events that follow insulin-receptor binding. The relative importance of each of these actions to the overall therapeutic effect of the drug will vary from patient to patient. Like glipizide, another second-generation sulfonylurea, glyburide exhibits mild diuretic actions but does not affect uric acid concentrations.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    In bioavailability studies of Glucovance 2.5 mg/500 mg and 5 mg/500 mg, the mean AUC for the glyburide component was 18% and 7%, respectively, greater than that of Micronase brand of glyburide coadministered with metformin. The glyburide component of Glucovance, therefore, is not bioequivalent to Micronase. Bioequivalence has not been established between Glucovance and other single-ingredient glyburide products. The metformin component is bioequivalent to single-ingredient metformin tablets. Food decreases the extent and rate of metformin absorption and also decreases the Tmax for glyburide from 7.5 hours to 2.75 hours. The clinical significance changes in pharmacokinetics occurring after administration of Glucovance with food is not known; the product is recommended to be taken with meals.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Specific pharmacokinetic studies have not been performed in those with hepatic dysfunction for either glyburide or metformin, but hepatic impairment may increase the risk of lactic acidosis.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Glyburide: Glyburide half-life increases with renal impairment and may lead to hypoglycemia. Generally use a conservative initial dose of glyburide in patients with CrCl 50 mL/minute or less.
Metformin: Metformin will accumulate in patients with CrCl less than 60 mL/minute and will increase the risk of lactic acidosis.The elderly, due to age-related decreased renal function, may accumulate metformin. Metformin is removed by hemodialysis. Initiation of metformin is not recommended in patients with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) between 30 and 45 mL/minute.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Metformin: The elderly, due to age-related decreased renal function, may accumulate metformin.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Gender does not appear to affect glyburide; metformin pharmacokinetics.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Race does not appear to affect glyburide; metformin pharmacokinetics.
  Text
  Glyburide; metformin is administered orally.
Glyburide: Once absorbed, glyburide is highly protein-bound via non-ionic binding, which differs from the ionic protein binding observed with first generation sulfonylureas. However, displacement from protein binding sites by other drugs may lead to enhanced hypoglycemic action of glyburide as with other sulfonylureas. Glyburide is metabolized completely in the liver to 2 weakly active metabolites that do not contribute significantly to clinical activity. Both unchanged drug and metabolites are excreted in the bile and urine; roughly 50% by each route; older sulfonylureas are primarily renally excreted. The terminal elimination half-life is roughly 10 hours, and the duration of action is 24 hours in adults with normal renal function.
Metformin: Once absorbed, metformin exhibits a large volume of distribution and is negligibly bound to plasma proteins. Metformin partitions into erythrocytes. Metformin appears to cross the placenta. Steady state is reached in 24 to 48 hours. The drug is not metabolized by the liver and this fact may explain why the risk of lactic acidosis is much less for metformin than for phenformin (i.e., approximately 10% of patients have an inherited defect in the ability to metabolize phenformin). Metformin is found excreted unchanged in the urine at a clearance 3.5 times that of CrCl, indicating a dependence on tubular secretion for the major route of elimination. Tubular secretion may be altered by many cationic drugs. Although the average elimination half-life in the plasma is 6.2 hours in patients with normal renal function, metformin accumulates in the red blood cells, which leads to a much longer elimination half-life in the blood (17.6 hours).
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: Organic cationic transporter-2 (OCT2), multidrug and toxin extrusion (MATE1 and MATE2k), CYP2C9, P-glycoprotein (P-gp)
Glyburide: Glyburide is a CYP2C9 substrate, and theoretically, CYP2C9 inhibitors or inducers may affect its metabolism. Glyburide is also a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp).
Metformin: Drugs that interfere with common renal tubular transport systems involved in the renal elimination of metformin (e.g., organic cationic transporter-2 [OCT2]/multidrug and toxin extrusion [MATE1 and MATE2k] inhibitors such as ranolazine, vandetanib, dolutegravir, and cimetidine) could increase systemic exposure to metformin. An interaction between metformin and oral cimetidine has been observed, with a 60% increase in peak metformin plasma and whole blood concentrations and a 40% increase in plasma and whole blood metformin AUC, but no change in metformin elimination half-life. Careful patient monitoring and dose adjustment of metformin and/or the potentially interfering drug is recommended with concurrent use.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Generally avoid use of this combination in hepatic impairment; hepatic disease increases the risk of metformin-associated lactic acidosis.
  Renal Impairment
  eGFR more than 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2: No dosage adjustment of metformin is needed. Obtain an eGFR at least annually in all patients taking metformin. Generally use a conservative initial dose of glyburide in patients with CrCl 50 mL/min or less, since it is renally excreted and drug accumulation may cause prolonged hypoglycemia in patients with renal impairment.
eGFR 30 to 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2: Initiation of glyburide; metformin is not recommended. Obtain an eGFR at least annually in all patients taking glyburide; metformin. In patients whose eGFR is initially greater than 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2, and then later falls below 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2, assess the benefits and risks of continuing treatment. Discontinue glyburide; metformin if the patient’s eGFR later falls below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2. Generally use a conservative initial dose of glyburide in patients with CrCl 50 mL/min or less, since it is renally excreted and drug accumulation may cause prolonged hypoglycemia in patients with renal impairment. The ADA and others suggest it is reasonable to decrease the metformin dose by 50% (or use one-half the maximum recommended dose) and monitor renal function every 3 months in those with an eGFR less than 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2. Do not initiate the drug in patients at this stage. Additional caution is required in patients with anticipated significant fluctuations in renal status or those at risk for abrupt deterioration in kidney function, based on previous history, other comorbidities, albuminuria, and medication regimen (e.g., potent diuretics or nephrotoxic agents).
eGFR less than 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2: Use is contraindicated.
 
Intermittent hemodialysis
Glyburide; metformin use is contraindicated. Metformin is dialyzable; hemodialysis will efficiently remove accumulated metformin in the case of drug-induced lactic acidosis, provided metformin is halted.
